```properties
settings/volume = Volume
```

- One `key = value` per line
- Keys use `/` as a namespace separator
- Whitespace around `=` is ignored
- Lines starting with `#` are comments

## Key naming conventions

| Prefix | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `characters/` | Character display names |
| `items/` | Item names and descriptions |
| `rooms/` | Room/scene interaction prompts |
| `quest/` | Quest names and status descriptions |
| `quest/.../tag/` | Quest tag stamps in the journal (`{quest Name}/tag/{tag}`) |
| `ui/` | Interface labels (menus, battle UI, cooking) |
| `settings/` | Settings screen labels |
| `prolog/` | Prologue narrative text |
| `meeting_murakami/` | Murakami quest dialogue |
| `recipe/` | Recipe names and descriptions |

## Real examples

From `mods/cthangover_game/locale/en.properties`:

```properties
# Character names
characters/player/marao = Marao
characters/enemies/wolf = Wolf
characters/enemies/werewolf_girl = Werewolf

# Items
items/food/ration/name = Ration
items/food/ration/desc = A daily ration unit for one person
items/food/wolf_meat/name = Wolf cutlet
items/food/wolf_meat/desc = Raw wolf meat

# Settings
settings/back = Back
settings/audio = Audio
settings/music = Music
settings/language = Language
settings/lang_en = English
settings/lang_ru = Russian

# Battle UI
ui/battle/victory = Victory
ui/battle/next = End turn

# Cooking UI
ui/cook/cook = Cook
ui/cook/close = Close
ui/cook/recipe_time = {0} minutes
```

Keys can include format placeholders (`{0}`, `{1}`) for dynamic data.

## Scenario locale files

Separate `scenarios_{lang}.properties` files contain dialogue lines:

```properties
rooms/kitchen/prompt = A room equipped as a kitchen
rooms/kitchen/opt_exit = Leave
rooms/kitchen/opt_cook = Cook
meeting_murakami/after_wolf/greeting = - Dear lady!
meeting_murakami/after_wolf/help = - Help me...
```

These are referenced in `.scenario` files via the `key=` parameter:

```scenario
text "- Dear lady!" key=meeting_murakami/after_wolf/greeting first=marao/think
```

## Checking keys and encodings

The developer mod `locale_atlas` maps every key of every installed mod per
language and lists only the keys that need attention: missing in some
language, empty, referenced but never declared (scenario `key=`, C#
`Translate`/`Tr`, JSON text fields, `"@key"` in collections, `.tscn` texts),
declared twice in one mod, plus collection texts without a `locale` entry.
It also checks the raw bytes of each locale file: valid UTF-8 (no UTF-16,
cp1251 bytes or U+FFFD), no control characters, no mojibake, BOM and line
endings.

```bash
godot --path . -- --locale-atlas --locale-out=res://tests/artifacts/locale_atlas
```

The report (`report.md`, `report.json`) names the file and line to add for
every gap. A `select ""` with no prompt is written without `key=`.

## Supported languages

The set of languages is fixed in code (`LanguageCode.Supported`), the same seven as
the site, and the settings dropdown lists exactly these, in this order:

| Settings language | Locale code |
|---|---|
| Russian | `ru` |
| English | `en` |
| Spanish | `es` |
| Chinese | `zh` |
| Japanese | `ja` |
| Korean | `ko` |
| Vietnamese | `vi` |

Only the primary subtag counts (`en-US`, `en_us` and `EN` are English). An
unsupported settings value falls back to `ru` with a warning. Every built-in
mod ships all seven; a mod may ship fewer, and its keys then show as raw keys in
the missing languages — the locale atlas reports such gaps as errors. The names
of the languages in the settings come from `menu_base/locale/languages_<lang>.properties`.

### Loader API

Two static methods on `LocaleLoader` drive loading:

- `LoadCurrentLanguage()` — reads the language from the saved settings, reads it
  through `LanguageCode.Normalize` (falling back to `ru`), and delegates to `LoadLocale`
- `LoadLocale(string locale)` — merges the `.properties` files for that locale
  across **all** loaded mods into one Godot `Translation`, replaces any previous
  translation for that locale on `TranslationServer`, and sets it as the active
  locale

`LoadLocale` can be called directly to switch languages programmatically.

## Adding a new language

Adding a language requires a code change, not just new files:

1. Add a mapping to `LocaleLoader.LangToLocale` (e.g. `{ "de", "de" }`)
2. Add the option to the language dropdown in `SettingsMenu`
3. Create `locale/{locale}.properties` (or any `*_{locale}.properties`) with UI text translations
4. Create scenario translation files following the same naming convention

## Adding a new locale key

1. Add the key to all language `.properties` files
2. Reference it in scenarios or UI code using the exact key string
3. If referenced from a `.scenario` file, place it in `scenarios_{lang}.properties`
4. If referenced from C# code, use `GodotTranslationProvider.Instance.Get("key")` — it returns `null` for untranslated keys (or Godot's `TranslationServer`)

===== DOC docs/mods/map/index.md: Map and Travel Zones =====
The world map is a full-screen overlay where the player picks a destination and travels there. A map is a set of **zones**: interactive areas drawn over the map background, each pointing at a scene. Zone geometry lives in mod JSON data, while the open/hidden state is runtime state that scenarios change through dedicated DSL actions and the save system persists.

## Directory structure

Map data is read from every loaded mod, in mod load order:

```filestree
mods/<mod_name>/
└── map/
    └── map.json        # any *.json in this folder is loaded
```

The base mod ships one file:

```filestree
mods/cthangover_game/
└── map/
    └── map.json        # "home" and "town_entry" zones + two labels
```

## Zone data format

Files use the standard `{"Items": [...]}` envelope. All coordinates are normalised `[0.0 .. 1.0]` against the displayed map background, which keeps layouts resolution-independent:

```jsonc
{
  "Items": [
    {
      "ID": "home",                         // unique zone id (also the DSL reference)
      "points": [[0.58, 0.26], [0.61, 0.25]], // polygon highlight + click area
      "title": "",                          // localisation key for the name pill
      "scene": "home_outside",              // target scene, passed to SceneManager
      "description": "mapzone/home/info",   // optional localisation key for the card text
      "image": "",                          // asset id of the marker icon (empty = no marker)
      "image_pos": [0.6, 0.2],              // normalised marker position
      "label_pos": [0.59, 0.15],            // optional free-standing text label position
      "locked": false,                      // authoring default: closed for travel
      "hidden": false                       // authoring default: absent from the map
    }
  ]
}
```

| Field | Type | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `ID` | `string` | Unique zone identifier; the key used by DSL actions and the save |
| `points` | `[x, y][]` | Polygon vertices, normalised `[0..1]`; drawn as a two-tone hatch and used as the generous click target (fewer than 3 points = no polygon) |
| `title` | `string` | Localisation key resolved via `TranslationServer.Translate` |
| `scene` | `string` | Logical scene name for the transition; empty or unknown scenes keep the zone closed (dimmed marker, blocked button) |
| `description` | `string` | Optional localisation key for the location card flavour text |
| `image` | `string` | Marker icon asset id resolved through the mod texture system |
| `image_pos` | `[x, y]` | Normalised marker position |
| `label_pos` | `[x, y]` | Optional position of a non-interactive text label |
| `locked` | `bool` | Authoring default for the closed state (`false` = enterable) |
| `hidden` | `bool` | Authoring default for visibility (`false` = drawn) |

### Zone kinds

- **Location** — has an `image` icon and a `scene`: clickable marker, polygon, location card, transition button.
- **Label (inscription)** — has `label_pos` but no `image`: a plain text caption naming a region. It is not clickable, does not open the card and is excluded from the zone counter.
- **Hidden zone** — `"hidden": true` or hidden at runtime: completely absent (no polygon, marker, label or counter entry) until revealed.

## Opening the map

The map is declared like any mod panel — in the manifest `ui` array:

```jsonc
{
  "id": "map",
  "kind": "panel",
  "slot": "ModBeforeUIPanel",
  "scene_path": "scenes/ui/map_widget.tscn",
  "button": { "icon": "map", "locale": "ui/map/title", "index": 10 },
  "priority": 10
}
```

`UiBuilder` instantiates the scene and hands a toolbar button to `ToolBox` (the HUD toolbar); pressing it toggles the panel through `UiRegistry`. The widget closes on `Escape`, on the header ✕ button, and automatically after a successful transition.

## Runtime state

Zone state has two layers:

- **Authoring defaults** — `locked` / `hidden` flags from the JSON above.
- **Runtime overrides** — sparse dictionaries owned by the `MapZoneService` singleton (`mods/map_base/src/MapZoneService.cs`), changed via `Open`/`Close`/`Show`/`Hide`/`ResetZone`.

The effective state is the override when present, otherwise the authoring default. Every change publishes a `MapZoneChangedEvent`, so an open map updates live when a scenario changes a zone. Extra rule shared by the game and the editor (`MapZoneRules`): a zone whose `scene` is empty or unregistered is always treated as locked, so authors can sketch future regions safely. The zone matching the active scene is marked *you are here* and cannot be entered again.

## Scenario DSL actions

Four actions change zone state from a scenario. Each takes the required `zone_id` parameter; unknown IDs are ignored with a warning, so scripts can reference zones added by other mods.

| Action | Parameters | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| `action map.open_zone` | `zone_id=Id` | Opens the zone for travel |
| `action map.close_zone` | `zone_id=Id` | Closes the zone for travel |
| `action map.show_zone` | `zone_id=Id` | Reveals a hidden zone on the map |
| `action map.hide_zone` | `zone_id=Id` | Removes the zone from the map until shown again |

```scenario
action map.open_zone zone_id=temple
action map.show_zone zone_id=temple_label
```

Typical authoring pattern: ship zones with `"locked": true` / `"hidden": true` and open them from story events; the overrides survive scene changes and play sessions.

## Saving

Only the sparse overrides are saved — not the effective state. `RuntimeSerializer` stores them as a `MapZones` blob in the core `__core__` save slot (`open` and `visible` dictionaries keyed by zone ID, see `MapZoneStateData`), and `MapZoneService.ImportState` restores them on load. Storing deltas means a mod update that changes its authoring defaults is respected for every zone the player never touched. Starting a new game resets all overrides (`AppServices` resettable `map_zones`).

Persistence is covered by an integration test that runs the real `SaveService` save → reset → load cycle (`tests/Cthangover.Tests.Integration/Map/MapZonePersistenceTestSetup.cs`).

## Map editor

The `map_editor` mod ships a visual map editor (`mods/map_editor/src/MapEditor/`, tool id `map_editor`). It edits the same zone JSON: a canvas with draggable polygon, marker and label handles, per-zone fields, load/save of `map/*.json` into the selected mod, and a game-like preview that renders the real `map_widget.tscn` with the unsaved data. The editor and the game share one style implementation (`MapZoneStyle`), so both views render zones identically; after saving, the running map reloads without a game restart.

See Creating a Custom Tool for how tools like this are registered.

## Code reference

| Component | Location |
|---|---|
| `MapWidget` | `mods/map_base/src/MapWidget.cs` — overlay behaviour, markers, location card |
| `MapZonesLayer` | `mods/map_base/src/MapZonesLayer.cs` — polygon hit-testing and painting |
| `MapMarker` | `mods/map_base/src/MapMarker.cs` — marker emblem (`scenes/ui/map_marker.tscn`) |
| `MapZone` | `mods/map_base/src/MapZone.cs` — JSON zone DTO |
| `MapZoneService` | `mods/map_base/src/MapZoneService.cs` — zone list + runtime overrides singleton |
| `IMapZoneService` | `Core/Actions/IMapZoneService.cs` — scenario-facing facade (`ctx.Map`) |
| `MapZoneRules` / `MapZoneStyle` | `mods/map_base/src/` — shared enterability rule and zone visuals |
| `Map*ZoneAction` | `mods/map_base/src/Actions/` — the four DSL actions |

===== DOC docs/mods/metadata/index.md: Scene Event Metadata =====
The `[SceneEvent]` attribute allows mod C# code to hook into scene lifecycle events declaratively, without needing manifest subscriptions.

## `[SceneEvent]` attribute

Place the attribute on a **class** in a mod assembly to register it for automatic execution. Only classes deriving from `ExecutableEvent` are registered (`Core/Scenes/SceneEventRegistry.cs` skips any other type):

```csharp
using Cthangover.Core.Scenes;
using Cthangover.Core.UI.Executable;

[SceneEvent("home_kitchen", Priority = 5)]
public class KitchenInitEvent : ExecutableEvent
{
    // Runs when home_kitchen scene loads, ordered within the
    // scene's event chain by its Priority / After metadata
}

[SceneEvent("town_entry", After = "other_event")]
public class TownEnterEvent : ExecutableEvent
{
    // Runs after the event whose ID is "other_event"
}
```

There is no method-based registration and no `SceneContext` parameter — the attribute cannot be applied to methods (`AttributeUsage(AttributeTargets.Class, AllowMultiple = false)`).

## Attribute parameters

| Parameter | Type | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `SceneName` | string | *(positional, required)* Target scene name |
| `Priority` | int | Execution order (lower = earlier). Default 0 |
| `After` | string | Name of another handler this should run after |
| `Condition` | string | Condition expression — handler only runs if expression is true |

## Registration

`SceneEventRegistry` uses reflection at assembly load time to discover all **classes** decorated with `[SceneEvent]`. Only types deriving from `ExecutableEvent` register; decorated classes without that base type are silently skipped. No manual registration is needed — the attribute itself serves as registration.

## Execution order

`[SceneEvent]` classes are not a separate phase: `SceneManager.ComposeEvents` composes them into the **same event chain** as `.scenario` definitions. The composition runs synchronously inside `SceneManager.SwitchScene` — it is **not** a bus subscriber — and the composed chain is enqueued right after the `scene_entered` lifecycle event is published. Engine subscribers of that event run at their own fixed priorities (background 0, ambient audio 5, music type 10, UI host 30), which are independent of scenario ordering. So the order on scene enter is:

1. `scene_entered` bus subscribers apply scene defaults, audio, and UI host changes
2. The composed chain executes: scenario + `[SceneEvent]` events interleaved and sorted by their own `priority` / `After` metadata

On scene exit, `scene_exited` is published on the bus; there are no per-scene exit hooks beyond its subscribers.

## vs manifest `ui` entries

| Feature | `[SceneEvent]` attribute | Manifest `ui` entry |
|---|---|---|
| Registration | Attribute on class (must derive from `ExecutableEvent`) | JSON entry in `manifest.json` |
| Purpose | Game logic running in the scene's event chain | UI panels/overlays anchored to shell slots |
| Scene binding | `SceneName` positional parameter (required) | Optional `scene` field — when set, the element is only available in that scene |
| Condition support | Yes (`Condition` param) | No |
| Priority ordering | Yes | Yes (order inside the anchor slot) |
| Dependencies (`After`) | Yes | No |

Use `[SceneEvent]` for game-logic hooks. Use manifest `ui` entries when you need to anchor a panel or overlay to a shell slot.

## Manifest `conflicts_with` — declarative only

The `conflicts_with` manifest field is **declarative only**: it is parsed (`Core/Mods/ModManifest.cs`) and exported to catalog entries, but the runtime does **not** enforce conflicts — no mods are disabled or blocked at load time. Treat it as informational metadata describing mutually exclusive mod pairs for catalogs and tooling.

## Naming your mod

The `id` tells other modders what role the mod plays, by a fixed suffix or
prefix, in lowercase `snake_case`. The folder name, `id`, the repository
(`mod_<id>`) and the locale keys `mod_<id>_name`/`mod_<id>_desc` match.

| Role | Pattern | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Engine subsystem other mods build on | `<subject>_base` | `dialog_base`, `ui_base`, `shell2d_base` |
| Mechanic, optional for a game | plain noun or `<variant>_<mechanic>` | `cooking`, `card_battle` |
| The game itself: scenes, story, entry point | `<game>_game` | `cthangover_game` |
| Further content of one game | `<game>_<part>` | `cthangover_murakami` |
| Reusable content any game may take | `<theme>_pack` | `town_pack` |
| Template to copy | `<what>_template` | the collection `novel_template` (its own mod: `my_novel_game`) |
| Developer tool | `<subject>_<tool>` | `editor_tools`, `resource_atlas` |
| Test fixture | `test_<what>` | `test_defaults_and_patches` |

Never use `core` in an id and never `base` as a prefix. The game a player
picks is not a mod but a collection package that lists
these mods.

## `manifest.json` field reference

Every mod's `manifest.json` is deserialized into `ModManifest` (`Core/Mods/ModManifest.cs`). All fields are optional:

| Field | Type | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `id` | string | Canonical mod identifier. Used for deduplication (when two mods declare the same `id`, the higher `version` wins) and in other mods' `depends`. Falls back to the filesystem entry name when absent |
| `name` | string | Human-readable mod name (shown in UI) |
| `version` | string | Semver mod version (e.g. `"1.0.0"`). Used by the save system to detect downgrades and trigger migrations |
| `author` | string | Author credit string |
| `description` | string | Short description of what the mod does |
| `category` | string | Optional catalog category, or a comma-separated combination for mods that belong to several groups (e.g. `"library, gameplay"`). Slugs: `story`, `characters`, `gameplay`, `visual`, `audio`, `ui`, `locale`, `library`, `engine`, `overhaul`, `tools`, `editor`, `debug`, or `other` (default). Parsed case-insensitively into the `[Flags]` `CategoryType`; all selected categories are pre-checked in the Catalog Export dialog (`CatalogExportWindow`) |
| `sources` | string[] | Glob-style paths to C# source files (e.g. `"src/"`, `"scripts/*.cs"`). Collected and compiled via Roslyn by `ModCompiler` |
| `gd_sources` | string[] | Glob-style paths to GDScript files (e.g. `"scripts/*.gd"`, `"gd/**/*.gd"`). `ModGDScriptLoader` collects them, compiles via Godot's GDScript runtime, and registers them as scenario actions or mod initializers |
| `test_sources` | string[] | Test-only C# globs, compiled separately in explicit mod-test mode |
| `test_suites` | array | Opt-in suites with `id`, optional `path` and activation prefixes |
| `integrations` | array | Conditional virtual mods: `id`, `with` partner IDs, optional `path`; see Integrations |
| `fixture` | bool | Test fixture excluded from ordinary game/all-mod selection |
| `depends` | string[] | Versioned dependencies in format `modId[op][version][?]` — see below. Parsed into `ModDependency` entries via `ModDependencyListConverter`; affects topological compile order and version validation |
| `depends_native` | string[] | Glob patterns for native shared libraries handled by `ModNativeLoader` — see below |
| `conflicts_with` | string[] | Mutually exclusive mod IDs. Declarative only — see section above |
| `ui` | array | GUI declarations. Each entry is a toolbar-toggled panel (`"kind": "panel"`) or a code-driven overlay (`"kind": "overlay"`) anchored to a named shell slot (`slot`), with an optional `scene` field scoping it to one scene |
| `main_menu` | array | Buttons for the title screen; each entry opens a fullscreen mod scene |

### `depends` — version syntax

Each `depends` entry is a string `[modId][op][version][?]`, parsed by `ModDependency.Parse` (`Core/Mods/ModDependency.cs`):

```jsonc
{
    "depends": [
        "cthangover_game",           // bare id — any version
        "cthangover_game>=1",        // core 1 or newer
        "my_lib^1.2.0",   // version starting with segments 1.2.0
        "extra_icons<2",
        "soft_dep?"       // trailing ? — optional dependency
    ]
}
```

| Operator | Meaning |
|---|---|
| *(none)* | Any version — bare `"modId"` |
| `>=` / `<=` / `>` / `<` | Per-segment comparison against the dependency's actual version |
| `=` | Exact match (segment-wise) |
| `^` | Prefix match — required segments must equal the leading segments of the actual version |

- A trailing `?` marks an **optional** dependency: if the mod is missing, that is not an error; if present, its version is still checked.
- Comparison goes through `VersionHelper.CompareSegments` (`Core/Utils/VersionHelper.cs`): both version strings are split on `.`, both segment arrays are padded with `"0"` to equal length, and each segment compares numerically if both parse as int, otherwise via ordinal string comparison. Segments may contain letters (`1a`, `alpha-3`, `1b.2.0`).
- Entries are read from JSON by `ModDependencyListConverter`: malformed strings are logged as errors, kept with `IsValid = false`, and reported later during `ValidateDependencies`.
- Valid dependency IDs also drive load order: `ModRegistry.GetOrderedModIds` topologically sorts so dependencies always precede their dependents.

### `depends_native` — native libraries

`depends_native` lists glob patterns (e.g. `"bin/*.dll"`, `"native/*.so"`, `"lib/*.dylib"`) for native shared libraries shipped inside the mod. `ModNativeLoader.LoadNativeDlls` (`Core/Mods/ModNativeLoader.cs`), called from `ModRegistry.Initialize()` before managed mod code runs:

1. Collects platform-appropriate files (`.dll` on Windows, `.so` on Linux, `.dylib` on macOS)
2. Copies them to the cache directory (`user://mod_cache/native/`) so zip-based mods can serve native code
3. Registers a `DllImportResolver` so P/Invoke (`[DllImport]`) declarations resolve against the mod-provided native code

If multiple mods provide the same library name, the first one wins; duplicates are logged as warnings. On reload, `ModRegistry.Reload()` calls `ModNativeLoader.FreeAll()` to release all native handles so the files can be overwritten on the next load cycle.

### Precompiled DLLs (`dll/` directory)

A mod may ship precompiled .NET assemblies in a `dll/` directory. During `ModRegistry.Initialize()`, `ModAssemblyLoader.LoadPrecompiledDlls` (`Core/Mods/ModAssemblyLoader.cs`) scans that directory recursively, copies each `.dll` to the assembly cache (`{modId}_dll_{fileName}`), loads it, and runs the standard `RegisterAssembly` fan-out (the same registries listed on the C# Source Code page).

This runs **before** Roslyn compilation of `sources`, so source-based mods can reference types from precompiled API assemblies. Each loaded precompiled assembly also gets a `DllImportResolver` installed (`ModNativeLoader.InstallResolverOnAssembly`) for its own P/Invoke calls.

| `scenario_tools_base` | Shared scenario file access, syntax and graph models for authoring tools. No tool windows. | Guide |
| `scene_builder` | Inspect and edit Godot scene nodes and their properties with a live preview. | Guide |
| `scene_editor` | Create and edit logical scenes, backgrounds, ambient sound and scenario bindings. | Guide |
| `schema_base` | Lets every mod describe its scenario actions, DSL commands and data fields as data; the tools build forms, pickers and checks from these descriptions. | Guide |
| `schema_tools` | The Data Editor window and the command palette: edit any described entity in a form and assemble checked scenario lines from the mods' schemas. | Guide |
| `shell2d_base` | The frame every 2D game is drawn in: the background view box, the toolbar, panel slots and notifications that content mods fill. | Guide |
| `skills_base` | The player's collection of skill cards: skills that open possibilities in other mechanics, grant battle action cards and add up into the collection effect. | Guide |
| `stat_checks` | One scenario command, check: roll a character's stat plus dice against a difficulty and branch the story on success or failure. | Guide |
| `stats_base` | A stats engine for any genre: stats, archetypes and traits as data, formulas for derived numbers, character sheets, levels and pools — handed to battles unchanged. | Guide |
| `test_defaults_and_patches` | A test fixture, not content: property patches the property_patches test loads to check how patches of other mods' characters and items merge. Never part of a game. | Guide |
| `test_integrations` | A test fixture, not content: hosts the integration 'probe' that joins a mod set only together with quests_base, so the tests can check both the boot and the in-process mounting gate. Never part of a game. | Guide |
| `tile_isometric_base` | Draws tile maps at an angle, a grid of 2:1 diamonds, so buildings and trees stand up out of the ground. One of the two views of Tile Rendering. | Guide |
| `tile_render_base` | The shared engine behind tile maps: turns a prepared map description into a picture; the viewing angle is a small renderer mod on top. | Guide |
| `tile_topdown_base` | Draws tile maps straight from above, on a square grid, in the style of classic 2D RPGs. One of the two views of Tile Rendering; RPG Plus uses it. | Guide |
| `time_base` | The in-game clock, day and night: scenes change their look with the time, lamps and windows light them, and a portable lamp helps after dark. | Guide |
| `tools_base` | Shared forms, file access, localization editing and UI components for authoring tools. No tool windows. | Guide |
| `town_pack` | Ready-made scenery for a small-town story: the town entrance, a home vestibule and an evening bar as backgrounds, with the bar's ambience. No story inside. | Guide |
| `trading` | Shops as data and a trade window of two inventories: the merchant's goods on one side, the player's bag on the other, and money as an ordinary item. | Guide |
| `turn_battle_base` | The platform under every turn-based battle: starting from a scenario, the battle scene, victory and defeat, experience and loot — how the player fights is a separate battle system. | Guide |
| `ui_base` | The interface layer every game shares: the toolbar, the in-game menu, the save indicator, notifications and the common look of panels, buttons and lists. | Guide |
| `validator` | Checks every mod without running the game: broken references, unknown scenario commands, wrong parameters, missing dependencies — a report on every game start. | Guide |
| `village_pack` | Reusable RPG art: nine tilesets, walking sprites, battle artwork, snow enemies, six shared architectural interiors, reusable containers and editable atmosphere profiles. | Guide |

## Games and toolsets

The four `kind: game` collections are `cthangover`, `novel_template`, `rpg_plus`, and `junior_reaper_paper_grief`. `frontend` is the menu/shell toolset; `dev_tools` groups authoring capabilities. Collections are packages under `collections/<id>/collection.json`, not mods.

`editor_tools` is a source-free compatibility package bringing seven independent editors. A game can choose individual tools instead. `tools_base` supplies common widgets/documents and neutral services; `scenario_tools_base` adds dialog-specific file and graph models. Neither registers a window of its own.

`rpg__battles` and `turn_battle_base__quests` are production integrations. Their host/partner combination determines whether they mount. Mod test suites and fixture mods instead require explicit test activation.

===== DOC docs/mods/musics/index.md: Music & Playlists =====
Background music tracks (`.ogg`) organized into playlists with ambient/combat categorization. Playlist selection happens per scene: the engine picks the playlist whose `Scene` matches the current scene name and switches between its categories based on game state (exploration vs battle).

## Directory structure

```filestree
music/
├── track_name_1.ogg
├── track_name_2.ogg
└── playlists/
    ├── default.json
    ├── battle.json
    └── town_entry.json
```

## File format

- `.ogg` — Vorbis-compressed audio, any bitrate
- Files are referenced by name (without extension) in playlists

## Real example — `music_pack/music/playlists/default.json`

```jsonc
{
  "Scene": "default",               // Scene name this playlist belongs to ("default" is the fallback)
  "Musics": [
    {
      "MusicType": "Ambient",       // Category: music for exploration/non-combat states
      "MusicNames": [               // Track filenames without .ogg extension
        "Abyssal Void",
        "Airborne Melody",
        "Binary Prelude",
        "Cinders of Dawn",
        "Melancholic Journey",
        "Nebula Pulse Echoes",
        "Quantum Archive",
        "Twilight Interlude"
      ]
    },
    {
      "MusicType": "Combat",        // Category: music switched to during battles
      "MusicNames": [
        "Aura of Decay",
        "Circuit Dreams II",
        "Crimson Echoes",
        "Crimson Horizon",
        "Glass Symphony",
        "Hex Doom Cart",
        "Iron Resonance",
        "Iron Resonance II",
        "Luminous Twilight",
        "Rusty Frequencies",
        "Skull Resonance",
        "Venom's Embrace",
        "Empty Save Room"
      ]
    }
  ]
}
```

8 ambient tracks for exploration, 13 combat tracks for battles — 21 total.

## Playlist JSON schema

```jsonc
{
  "Scene": "scene_name",              // Scene name this playlist targets ("default" = fallback for any scene)
  "Musics": [
    {
      "MusicType": "Force | Combat | Ambient",  // Category: Ambient (explore), Combat (battle), Force (transient)
      "MusicNames": ["track_1", "track_2", "..."]   // Track filenames without .ogg extension
    }
  ]
}
```

| Field | Required | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `Scene` | Yes | Scene name the playlist applies to (`"default"` is used when no playlist matches the scene) |
| `MusicType` | Yes | Category: `Ambient`, `Combat`, `Force`. `Force` is transient — normalised to `Ambient` |
| `MusicNames` | Yes | List of track filenames (no extension, no path) |

These are the only supported fields. There is no shuffle flag — a random track
is picked each time playback starts/advances. Fading is hard-coded in the
engine (~1s delay, then a volume ramp) and cannot be configured per playlist.

## Usage in scenarios

The `music`, `music_play`, and `music_pause` commands control music playback:

```scenario
# Play a single track by id (resolved via MusicFactory, as Ambient type)
music tavern_theme

text "A bard strums a melancholy tune..."

# Pause everything
music_pause

# Resume
music_play
```

Note that `music <id>` plays one track — it does not switch playlists.
Playlist selection is driven by scenes, not by scenario commands.

## Scene default ambient

Scene definitions can specify a `defaultAmbient` — but this is an **ambient
sound loop**, not a playlist. It is played via `AudioService.PlayAmbient(id)`
through the sound system (`SoundFactory`, group `"sounds"`), separate from the
music playlist system:

```jsonc
{
  "name": "town_entry",                                 // Scene ID
  "defaultBackground": "town/entry",                    // Background image path
  "defaultAmbient": "locations/wind",                   // Ambient sound id from sounds/
  "defaultScenario": "scenarios/town/town_entry_default.scenario"  // Default scenario on enter
}
```

Music playlists are matched to scenes automatically by scene name (see below),
not via `defaultAmbient`.

## Adding a new track

1. Place the `.ogg` file in `music/`
2. Add its name (without `.ogg`) to the desired playlist's `MusicNames` array
3. Restart the game — tracks are loaded at startup

## How playlists are selected

- `PlaylistFactory.CreatePlaylist(sceneName)` collects every `music/playlists/*.json`
  across all mods and uses those whose `Scene` matches the current scene name;
  if none match, it falls back to `Scene: "default"`
- When several mods define the same `MusicType` for a matched scene, the last
  loaded mod **replaces** that entire category's track list (last one wins)
- The music type is chosen per scene through the scene-route registry
  (`SceneRouteRegistry`): a mod registers a logical scene name together with a
  `MusicType` — `turn_battle_base` registers `Battle` → `Combat` — and any
  scene without a registered route plays `Ambient` tracks

## `MusicPlayerBehaviour` public API

`Cthangover.Core.Audio.MusicPlayerBehaviour` (`mods/audio_base/src/MusicPlayerBehaviour.cs`)
is the node that drives playlist auto-advance and scene-aware switching. Its
public surface:

| Member | Behaviour |
|---|---|
| `IsCanAutoPlay` | Top-level toggle for the auto-advance state machine; when `false` no tracks are picked and scene switches don't trigger playback |
| `EnabledAutoPlay()` | Re-enables auto-play and resumes the last saved track |
| `DisabledAutoPlay()` | Saves the current playback position, disables auto-play, stops playback |
| `UpdateMusicType(scene)` | Scene-transition entry point (`GodotSceneType` or string overload); orchestrates the Combat ↔ Ambient handoff |
| `PlayMusic(name[, stream][, isLooped])` | Plays a track by name (stream resolved via `MusicFactory`) or with an explicit `AudioStream`; the `isLooped` parameter is accepted but currently unused |
| `PauseMusic()` / `StopMusic()` | Pause in-place via `StreamPaused` vs. full stop (stream reference kept for restart) |
| `FadeMusic()` | Hard-coded fade-out: 1s delay, then a 6-second volume ramp to silence, then stop and reset bus volume |
| `NextSound()` | Picks a random track from the current playlist's category bucket, avoiding an immediate repeat when more than one track exists |

When combat ends, the ambient track that was interrupted is resumed from its
saved position — if that position is greater than 0.5s,
`OggPacketParser.CreateTrimmedStream` builds a truncated OGG stream starting at
the saved time instead of seeking.

## Creating a new playlist

1. Create `music/playlists/my_scene.json`
2. Set `Scene` to the exact scene name the playlist targets
3. Define `MusicType` and `MusicNames` entries — the engine picks the playlist up automatically when that scene loads

===== DOC docs/mods/musics/playlists/index.md: Playlists — JSON Reference =====
Detailed schema for music playlist configuration JSON files.

## Full schema

```jsonc
{
  "Scene": "scene_name",                // Scene name this playlist targets ("default" = fallback for any scene)
  "Musics": [
    {
      "MusicType": "Force | Combat | Ambient",  // Category: Ambient (explore), Combat (battle), Force (transient)
      "MusicNames": ["track1", "track2", "..."]   // Track filenames without .ogg extension
    }
  ]
}
```

These are the only supported fields. There is no shuffle flag — a random track
is picked each time playback starts or advances. Fading is hard-coded in the
engine (~1s delay, then a volume ramp) and cannot be configured per playlist.

## Field details

| Field | Type | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `Scene` | string | Scene name this playlist applies to (`"default"` is used when no playlist matches the loaded scene) |
| `MusicType` | string | Category: `Ambient` (exploration), `Combat` (battle), `Force` (transient, normalised to `Ambient`). The registered scene route chooses the music type (`turn_battle_base` supplies the battle route); ordinary scenes use `Ambient` |
| `MusicNames` | string[] | Track filenames without extension. Must match `.ogg` files in `music/` |

## Real example — default playlist

```jsonc
{
  "Scene": "default",               // Scene name; "default" is the fallback playlist
  "Musics": [
    {
      "MusicType": "Ambient",       // Exploration music
      "MusicNames": [
        "Abyssal Void", "Airborne Melody", "Binary Prelude",
        "Cinders of Dawn", "Melancholic Journey",
        "Nebula Pulse Echoes", "Quantum Archive", "Twilight Interlude"
      ]
    },
    {
      "MusicType": "Combat",        // Battle music — auto-switched by engine
      "MusicNames": [
        "Aura of Decay", "Circuit Dreams II", "Crimson Echoes",
        "Crimson Horizon", "Glass Symphony", "Hex Doom Cart",
        "Iron Resonance", "Iron Resonance II", "Luminous Twilight",
        "Rusty Frequencies", "Skull Resonance", "Venom's Embrace",
        "Empty Save Room"
      ]
    }
  ]
}
```

## Multiple playlists example

A single playlist can cover different states by defining multiple `MusicType` buckets:

```jsonc
// music/playlists/tavern.json — applies to the scene named "tavern"
{
  "Scene": "tavern",
  "Musics": [
    { "MusicType": "Ambient", "MusicNames": ["Tavern_Day", "Tavern_Night"] },
    { "MusicType": "Combat", "MusicNames": ["Tavern_Fight_01", "Tavern_Fight_02"] }
  ]
}
```

## Scene integration

Playlist selection happens per scene, automatically. When a scene loads,
`PlaylistFactory.CreatePlaylist(sceneName)` uses the playlist whose `Scene`
matches the scene name (falling back to `"default"`):

```jsonc
{
  "name": "town_tavern"    // Scene ID — playlists with "Scene": "town_tavern" apply here
}
```

Note: `defaultAmbient` in a scene JSON is unrelated to playlists — it plays an
ambient sound loop via the sound system (`AudioService.PlayAmbient`), not music.

The scenario command `music <id>` also does not switch playlists; it plays a
single track by id.

## How the engine switches playlists

1. Scene loads → `PlaylistFactory.CreatePlaylist(sceneName)` picks the playlist whose `Scene` matches (fallback `"default"`) → starts playing `Ambient` tracks
2. The music type comes from the scene-route registry (`SceneRouteRegistry`): `turn_battle_base` registers the `Battle` route with `MusicType` `Combat` → tracks come from that playlist's `Combat` bucket; scenes without a route (and the return to them) play `Ambient`
3. Scene changes → the new scene's playlist takes over, with the hard-coded fade
4. Multi-mod note: if several mods define playlists for the same scene, the last loaded mod replaces each `MusicType` bucket entirely (last one wins)

Playback always picks a random track from the active category bucket — there is
no ordered or shuffle-flagged mode.

Music types: `Force`, `Combat`, `Ambient`. Mixer buses (`MixerType`): `Sounds`,
`Musics`, `Ambient` — music plays through the dedicated `Musics` bus.

===== DOC docs/mods/patches/arrays-and-identity.md: Array operations and declared identity =====
The patch engine operates on JSON trees at any depth. Object properties merge recursively; scalars and bare arrays replace the previous value. A null patch value removes the property, so deserialization sees its default. `Key$set` replaces even an object wholesale.

| Patch key | Behaviour |
|---|---|
| `Skills` or `Skills$set` | Replace the array |
| `Skills$add` | Append; with a declared identity, merge an existing matching element instead |
| `Skills$remove` | Remove by declared identity, otherwise by semantic JSON value |

```json
{"Items": [{"Id": "Marao", "Skills$add": ["cook"], "Skills$remove": ["block"]}]}
```

Unkeyed additions are not deduplicated. JSON object property order does not change semantic equality. A missing array can be created by `$add`; removing from a missing array does nothing. Incompatible operations are diagnosed with a path inside the entity.

## Identity is explicit

The engine does **not** guess an identity from fields named `Id`, `ItemId` or `Name`. Keys come from these sources, in priority order:

1. An explicit operation value `{"$key": ..., "$items": [...]}`.
2. `[MergeKey("Id")]` on the typed DTO's list property.
3. `IMergeable.MergeKeyProperty` on the typed list element.

The type walk follows nested objects, lists and dictionaries, respecting JSON property names. Unknown extension-data fields have no DTO metadata; use an explicit `$key` there. A key can be one property or a composite list. Key names and string values compare case-insensitively.

For example, a mod-defined reward array must declare its key when updating one reward:

```json
{
  "Items": [{
    "Id": "daily_hunt",
    "Rewards$add": {"$key": "Id", "$items": [{"Id": "gold", "Count": 200}]},
    "Rewards$remove": {"$key": "Id", "$items": ["exp"]}
  }]
}
```

`$add` deep-merges only supplied fields into an element with the same key; it appends when the key is absent. `$remove` accepts an object with the key fields, or the bare value for a single-property key. A composite operation uses, for example, `"$key": ["Layer", "X", "Y"]`, and supplies all key fields in each item.

Known declarations include RPG object `Parts` and `States`, map `Strokes` and `Events` (key `Id`), and map `Lights` (key `id`). `LootEntry` uses its `IMergeable` key `ItemId`. Shop offers do not declare a key: use `"Sells$add": {"$key": "Item", "$items": [...]}` to update an existing price rather than append a duplicate.

## Layers applied later

`Variants`, placement overrides and state components can store patches that will only apply when the effective object is built. `[PatchLayer(typeof(...))]` tells the engine to compose such layers rather than execute their operations prematurely. For example, adding to `Interact.Commands` in a placement patch must still add to the object's commands when that placement is resolved.

Use the editors' field patches or preserve the stored layer structure; a fully resolved object is not the same as its stored inheritance layer.

## Deletion, isolation and API

An entity patch with `"$delete": true` removes the entity. A null property removes that property; it does not delete the entity. Wildcard patches run first, then targeted patches, in mod order within each group. The resolver applies each patch to a copy and verifies deserialization. A failing patch is logged with its mod, file, entity and JSON path and is skipped without discarding other valid patches.

`JsonPatchMerger.Apply(JsonObject target, JsonObject patch, Type targetType)` mutates a JSON object; callers applying it directly must handle rollback themselves. `JsonPatchMerger.Merge(...)` applies grouped patches and accepts an optional target DTO type. `MergeTypeMap` supplies nested type/key metadata. The former `DetectIdentityKey` and `BuildMergeKeys<T>` helpers are not current APIs.

Implementation: `Core/Mods/Patches/JsonPatchMerger.cs`, `Core/Mods/Patches/MergeTypeMap.cs`, `Core/Mods/Patches/PatchLayerComposer.cs` and `Core/Mods/Resolvers/Impls/PatchResolver.cs`.

===== DOC docs/mods/patches/index.md: Patches =====
Patches let one mod add or override properties of entities defined in another mod — without rewriting their JSON files entirely.

- **`"Id": "EntityName"`** — apply only to the entity with this ID.
- **`"Id": "*"`** — wildcard: applies to **all** entities of this type. Its values overwrite the entity's own JSON values; a targeted patch (`"Id": "EntityName"`) is applied after the wildcard and wins over it.

Works for every entity kind the game defines — `character`, `item`, `action`, `skill`, `quest`, `recipe`, `status_effect` — keyed by kind, not by directory.

## Overview

Each mod can contain an optional `patches/` directory. Patch sources are
collected by `PatchResolver` (`Core/Mods/Resolvers/Impls/PatchResolver.cs`),
which scans **both** layouts per entity kind:

```filestree
mods/
└── my_mod/
    └── patches/
        ├── character.json          ← flat file: patches for characters
        ├── item.json               ← flat file: patches for items
        └── character/              ← directory form: any *.json inside is scanned too
            ├── player.json
            └── enemies.json
```

- Flat files: `patches/{kind}.json` (e.g. `patches/character.json`)
- Directory form: every `.json` inside `patches/{kind}/`

Both forms use the same format below; results from all mods are grouped by
target entity id. From C#, patches are collected via the static facade
`Patches.Collect(kind)`, which proxies through
`ModManager.Instance.GetResolver<PatchResolver>()` (results are cached per
kind until `ClearCache()`). The kind is the entity kind declared in the target
file's envelope (`"Kind": "character"`), so a character patch is never applied
to skills or actions.

## Format

Standard `{"Items": [...]}` wrapper. Each entry contains an `Id` and arbitrary properties to add:

```jsonc
{
  "Items": [
    {
      "Id": "Marao",                    // Id matching: applies only to entity with this exact Id
      "TestPatched": 42,                // Arbitrary property added via patch
      "TestDefault": 100                // Will be layered over wildcard values
    },
    {
      "Id": "*",                        // Wildcard: applies to ALL entities of the kind
      "TestDefault": -20,               // Overridden by specific patches (overwrites the entity's own value)
      "TestGlobalPatch": 1              // Global property applied to all entities
    }
  ]
}
```

## Property Resolution Order

When creating an entity, its properties are assembled in layers. Each subsequent layer overwrites keys from the previous ones:

| Layer | Source | Priority |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Entity's own properties (from its JSON) | Base |
| 2 | Wildcard patches `"*"` (in mod load order) | Higher |
| 3 | Targeted patches by `Id` (in mod load order) | Highest |

Property names match in any letter case (`"health"` patches `Health`), the same way entity JSON is read.

## Patches from Different Mods

Patches **accumulate**: each mod adds its entries to the list for the target ID. When applied, they are iterated in load order, and a later mod overwrites keys of an earlier one.

Mod A:
```jsonc
// Wildcard patch adds Test and Strength to all characters
{ "Items": [{ "Id": "*", "Test": -20, "Strength": 10 }] }
```

Mod B (loaded after A):
```jsonc
// Mod B (loaded after A): overwrites Test but preserves Strength
{ "Items": [{ "Id": "*", "Test": 0 }] }
```

Result for all characters: `{ "Test": 0, "Strength": 10 }` — `Test` overridden, `Strength` preserved.

## Full Example

### Mod A — defines a character

```jsonc
// mods/mod_a/characters/player/hero.json
{
  "Id": "Hero",           // Unique entity identifier for patch targeting
  "Health": 100,          // Base properties defined by the entity itself
  "Attack": 20,
  "Strength": 15
}
```

### Mod B — adds patches

```jsonc
// mods/mod_b/patches/character.json
{
  "Items": [
    { "Id": "Hero", "Test": 50, "CritChance": 15 },                    // Specific patch: applies only to Hero
    { "Id": "*", "Test": -20, "CritChance": 5, "GlobalBuff": 10 }      // Wildcard: applies to every character; Hero gets its own values above
  ]
}
```

### Result for character `Hero`

| Property | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| `Strength` | 15 | Entity properties |
| `Test` | **50** | Specific patch `Hero` overrode wildcard `-20` |
| `CritChance` | **15** | Specific patch `Hero` overrode wildcard `5` |
| `GlobalBuff` | **10** | Wildcard patch `*` |

### Result for `Wolf` (no specific patch)

| Property | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| `Test` | **-20** | Wildcard `*` |
| `CritChance` | **5** | Wildcard `*` |
| `GlobalBuff` | **10** | Wildcard `*` |

## Items

Everything works identically for items. `ItemInfo` accepts arbitrary JSON keys via `[JsonExtensionData]`, which end up in `Item.Properties` (mirroring characters via `CharacterAttributes.Properties`).

```jsonc
// patches/item.json
{
  "Items": [
    { "Id": "food/ration", "Durability": 99, "SpecialProp": true },  // Specific item patch by Id
    { "Id": "*", "Durability": 50, "GlobalItemProp": "patched" }      // Wildcard: fallback for all items
  ]
}
```

## Test fixture

`mods/test_defaults_and_patches/` is a test fixture (`"fixture": true` in its manifest): `--all-mods` and games
never load it, so its wildcard patches cannot leak into other tests. The `property_patches` integration test
loads it explicitly and checks the merged entities:

```bash
godot --path . --headless -- --with-mods=test_defaults_and_patches --itest=property_patches
```

```filestree
mods/test_defaults_and_patches/
├── manifest.json
├── src/PropertyPatchesTest.cs   ← itest driver
└── patches/
    ├── character.json    ← Maraо: TestPatched=42, TestDefault=100; *: TestDefault=-20, TestGlobalPatch=1
    └── item.json         ← food/ration: Durability=99; *: Durability=50, GlobalItemProp=patched
```

## Array Operations & Public Helpers

Besides scalar last-wins overwrites, patch keys can carry array operation
suffixes — `$add`, `$remove`, and `$set` (parsed by
`JsonPatchMerger`). These enable appending, removing, and
explicitly replacing array elements, including per-field merging of object
arrays matched by identity keys. See
Array Operations & Identity Keys for the full
reference.

Mechanically, `JsonPatchMerger.Merge` applies all patches whose entry id is
`"*"` (the wildcard targeting every entity of the type) first, then the
patches matching the concrete entity id.

Public APIs are `JsonPatchMerger.Apply(JsonObject, JsonObject, Type)` and `Merge(..., Type targetType = null)`. `MergeTypeMap` walks DTO types for declared list identities. There is no identity-name guessing: extra arrays use explicit `$key` / `$items` operation values. Null removes a property, `$set` replaces an object wholesale, and `"$delete": true` removes an entity. See the array reference for nested layers and failure isolation.

## Pages

- Array Operations & Identity Keys — $add/$remove/$set, per-field merge, declared identities and explicit keys
- Scene Patches — patching `.tscn` scenes with inherited scenes and `metadata/_patch_*` operations

===== DOC docs/mods/patches/scenes.md: Scene Patches =====
Scene patches let one mod modify a `.tscn` scene shipped by another mod or by the game itself — without copying and rewriting the whole file. A patch is a regular Godot **inherited scene**: it instances the target and lists only the changes. Godot's inheritance format already expresses property overrides, added nodes, sibling order, groups and connections; the few operations it cannot express (deleting nodes, deleting inherited properties, bulk edits, resource cleanup) are encoded with reserved `metadata/_patch_*` keys in the same file.

This is a separate subsystem from entity patches: the target is a scene path, not an entity kind.

## Patch file

Patches live in a mod under `patches/scenes/`, recursively:

```filestree
mods/
└── my_mod/
    └── patches/
        └── scenes/
            └── menu/
                └── main_menu.tscn      ← patch for scenes/menu/main_menu.tscn
```

The target is declared in the file itself — an inherited-scene header plus a root node that instances the target:

```ini
[gd_scene format=3]

[ext_resource type="PackedScene" path="res://mods/my_mod/scenes/menu/main_menu.tscn" id="1_target"]

[node name="MainMenuScene" instance=ExtResource("1_target")]
```

All three spellings of the same scene resolve to one target: the resolver key (`scenes/menu/main_menu.tscn`), the native form (`res://scenes/menu/main_menu.tscn`) and the on-disk form of a mod scene (`res://mods/<mod>/scenes/...`).

## Layers

Patches are applied on top of the scene that won the override battle in `TscnSceneResolver`:

1. The scene's own file (later mods override earlier ones entirely).
2. Patch layers in **mod load order**.
3. Inside one mod, patch files apply in **path order** — use `00-`, `10-` prefixes when the order matters.

Every layer sees the result of the previous ones, so a later mod can override a value set by an earlier patch.

## Cross-mod base scene (shell2d_base + dialog_base + rpg)

`shell2d_base` owns the 2D shell scene; engine mods add their layers to it with a patch instead of shipping their own shell. Two mods of different genres extend the same shell:

```filestree
mods/
├── shell2d_base/
│   └── scenes/ui/base_scene.tscn           ← BaseScene / Scene / ViewBox / TopView / slots / notifications
├── dialog_base/
│   └── patches/scenes/ui/base_scene.tscn   ← DialogCanvas over the view box
└── rpg/
    └── patches/scenes/ui/base_scene.tscn   ← RpgWorldHost (the tile world) between the view box and the dialog
```

```ini
[gd_scene load_steps=3 format=3]

[ext_resource type="PackedScene" path="res://mods/shell2d_base/scenes/ui/base_scene.tscn" id="1_target"]
[ext_resource type="PackedScene" path="res://scenes/ui/dialog_canvas.tscn" id="2_dialog"]

[node name="BaseScene" instance=ExtResource("1_target")]

[node name="DialogCanvas" parent="Scene" index="2" instance=ExtResource("2_dialog")]
```

The base shell is a single flat scene, so `index="2"` inserts the dialog exactly between `ViewBox` and `TopView`. Patches cascade in load order: `rpg` depends on `dialog_base`, so its `RpgWorldHost` with the same `index="2"` lands before the `DialogCanvas`, and dialogs stay on top of the map. Later mods can override or delete shell nodes the same way. The shell contract mods rely on: root `BaseScene` in group `main_event_chain`, `Scene/ViewBox`, `Scene/TopView`, slots `ModFirstPanel` / `ModBeforeUIPanel` / `ModLastPanel`, and `Lastground` inside the view box. Both the target path and the added resource paths may use the editor's `res://mods/<mod>/...` form — it is normalised to the mod-relative key at load time.

## Operations

| Operation | How to write it | Editor |
|---|---|---|
| Override a property | node without `type`, `key = value` | yes |
| Override the root | properties on the root `instance=` node | yes |
| Add a node / subtree | node with `type=` | yes |
| Add a scene instance | node with `instance=ExtResource(...)` | yes |
| Replace a script | `script = ExtResource(...)` on an override node | yes |
| Reorder siblings | `index="N"` in the node heading | yes |
| Replace groups | `groups=[...]` in the node heading | yes |
| Add/remove groups | `metadata/_patch_add_groups` / `metadata/_patch_remove_groups` | no |
| Rebind exported node paths | `node_paths=[...]` + `NodePath(...)` values | yes |
| Add a connection | `[connection ...]` | yes |
| Delete a node | `metadata/_patch_delete = true` on the override node | no |
| Delete nodes by mask | root: `metadata/_patch_delete_nodes = PackedStringArray("Panel/*Btn")` | no |
| Delete a property | `metadata/_patch_remove = "tooltip_text"` | no |
| Delete properties by mask | `metadata/_patch_remove = PackedStringArray("theme_override_*")` | no |
| Delete a connection | root: `metadata/_patch_remove_connections = PackedStringArray("from=Panel/Btn;signal=pressed")` | no |
| Add resources | normal `[ext_resource]` / `[sub_resource]` sections | yes |
| Delete a sub_resource | `metadata/_patch_delete = true` in its body | no |
| Delete an ext_resource | root: `metadata/_patch_remove_ext_resources = PackedStringArray("1_tex")` | no |
| Change the entry point | `__entry_point = "My.Namespace.EntryPoint"` on the root | no |
| Resource garbage collection | root: `metadata/_patch_gc = true` | no |

A patch adds nodes when the node heading has `type=`/`instance=`; a heading without them overrides the existing node at that path. An override for a path that does not exist is logged and skipped.

## Deleting things

```ini
[node name="TelegramLink" parent="."]
metadata/_patch_delete = true

[node name="LoadBtn" parent="MenuContainer"]
metadata/_patch_remove = PackedStringArray("theme_override_*")
```

- `_patch_delete` removes the node, its descendants, connections touching the subtree and `NodePath` bindings that point into it.
- The scene root can never be deleted — the layer is rejected with an error.
- Paths are relative to the root and never include the root name (`MenuContainer/LoadBtn`, not `MainMenuScene/MenuContainer/LoadBtn`).
- Masks use `*` (any run of characters, including `/`) and `?` (one character). `_patch_delete_nodes = PackedStringArray("*")` removes every descendant and keeps the root.
- Deleting a property from the scene means the builder no longer applies it, so the Godot default remains. Godot has no real "unset".

## Connections

Add a connection with a normal section:

```ini
[connection signal="pressed" from="MenuContainer/LoadBtn" to="." method="_on_load_pressed"]
```

Remove one with a selector on the root — `from`, `to`, `signal`, `method` and `flags` are supported, omitted fields are wildcards, and every matching connection is removed:

```ini
[node name="MainMenuScene" instance=ExtResource("1_target")]
metadata/_patch_remove_connections = PackedStringArray(
    "from=MenuContainer/LoadBtn;signal=pressed",
    "to=.;signal=quit"
)
```

At least `signal` and one of `from`/`to` are required so a typo cannot silently wipe every connection.

## Resources

Patch resources are merged into the target:

- the same `type`+`path` already exists → the existing resource is reused;
- the id is free → it is copied as is;
- the id is taken by a different resource → the new resource gets a `_pN` suffix and all references in the patch are rewritten automatically.

`_patch_gc = true` removes ext/sub_resources that nothing references any more. GC runs **once after all layers**, so a resource that a later mod still references survives.

## Editor workflow

Create the patch with **File → New Inherited Scene**, pick the target, make your changes and save it as `.tscn` under `patches/scenes/`. Property overrides, added nodes, sibling order, groups, scripts and connections are authored normally. The operations Godot does not support in inherited scenes (deleting nodes, deleting properties, masks) are written by hand as the metadata keys above — adding metadata to an inherited root persists on save.

## Debugging

Patch layers are invisible in the editor: when several mods patch the same scene, each sees only the base scene. Use `ScenePatchDebug.DumpEffective(scene, label)` (or the `TSCN` log category) to print the effective tree the builder receives. Invalid patches are logged with `TSCN` errors/warnings and skipped, so one broken patch cannot break scene loading.

## Limitations

- **Renaming nodes is not supported.** Other mods address nodes by path; a rename would silently move every override to a non-existent path. Delete and re-add instead.
- Binary `.scn`/`.escn` scenes cannot be patched.
- Connections with `to` other than the scene root are filtered by the runtime builder, for patches as well.
- Array values are replaced as a whole; element-wise `$add`/`$remove` operations are not supported.
- Patching a native scene makes it build through the runtime TSCN pipeline from then on; scenes without patches keep the engine loader.

===== DOC docs/mods/quests/index.md: Quests =====
Quest definitions with status-based progression and tag-based conditions that control which scenarios execute.

## JSON structure

```jsonc
{
  // Entity kind — routes the file to the quest collector
  "Kind": "quest",
  // All quests of the file are listed under Items
  "Items": [{
    // Unique quest identifier (PascalCase); used in scenario condition expressions
    "Id": "QuestId",
    // Locale key prefix for the quest's display name in the journal
    "Name": "quest/locale/prefix",
    // Maps integer status codes to locale keys for journal descriptions at each stage
    "StatusToDescription": {
      "0": "locale_key_for_status_0",
      "1": "locale_key_for_status_1",
      "2": "locale_key_for_status_2"
    }
  }]
}
```

## Field reference

| Field | Description |
|---|---|
| `Id` | Unique quest identifier (PascalCase). Used in scenario `condition:` expressions |
| `Name` | Locale key prefix for the quest's display name |
| `StatusToDescription` | Map of status integers to locale keys for the journal description at each stage |

## Real examples

### Awakening quest — `cthangover_game/quests/quests.json`

```jsonc
{
  "Kind": "quest",
  "Items": [{
    // Quest ID — checked in scenario condition expressions
    "Id": "AwakeningQuest",
    // Locale key prefix → "Awakening"
    "Name": "quest/awakening",
    // One journal entry per prologue chapter; the journal strikes through
    // past entries and highlights the current `quest.Data.Status`
    "StatusToDescription": {
      "0": "quest/awakening/0",
      "1": "quest/awakening/1",
      "2": "quest/awakening/2",
      "3": "quest/awakening/3",
      "4": "quest/awakening/4",
      "5": "quest/awakening/5"
    }
  }]
}
```

### Old House quest — `cthangover_game/quests/quests.json`

Opened when the hero reaches the villa; the house arc is not written yet, so
the quest deliberately has no `StatusToDescription` — the journal shows its
name with the "pages are still empty" placeholder:

```jsonc
{
  "Kind": "quest",
  "Items": [{
    "Id": "OldHouseQuest",
    "Name": "quest/old_house"
  }]
}
```

### Meeting Murakami quest — `murakami/quests/quests.json`

```jsonc
{
  "Kind": "quest",
  "Items": [{
    // Quest ID — used in scenario conditions like MeetingMurakamiQuest.hasTag(...)
    "Id": "MeetingMurakamiQuest",
    // Locale key prefix → "The Girl at the Edge of Town"
    "Name": "quest/meeting/murakami",
    // Status descriptions for each quest stage
    "StatusToDescription": {
      "0": "quest/meeting/murakami/0",
      "1": "quest/meeting/murakami/1",
      "2": "quest/meeting/murakami/2"
    }
  }]
}
```

## Two kinds of status — don't conflate them

A quest carries **two independent state values** (`mods/quests_base/src/Quests/`, with transitional contracts in `Core/Quests/`):

| Where | Type | What it means | How it changes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `QuestBase.Status` | `QuestStatus` enum (`byte`) | Quest **lifecycle** — not started / active / finished | `quest.set_status` → `QuestService.SetStatus` |
| `QuestData.Status` (i.e. `quest.Data.Status`) | raw `int` | Numeric **stage counter** used for journal descriptions and fine-grained progress | `quest.set_data_status level=N` → `QuestService.SetDataStatus` |

They are stored and mutated separately. Setting one does not touch the other.

### QuestStatus enum

`QuestStatus` (`Core/Quests/QuestStatus.cs`) is a byte-sized enum with exactly
three values:

| Name | Value | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `NotStarted` | 0 | Never accepted or activated; hidden from the journal by default |
| `Progress` | 1 | Accepted, possibly with sub-objectives tracked via tags |
| `End` | 2 | Terminal completed state |

`action quest.set_status` expects the **status names** above as the `status`
parameter; parsing via `Enums<QuestStatus>.Parse` is case-insensitive
(`notstarted`, `Progress`, `END` all work). Invalid names are logged and the
call is ignored.

## Quest tags

Quests use a tag system to track progress. Tags are set via scenario `action` commands:

```scenario
action battle.init scene=town_entry enemies=wolf_2,werewolf_girl_1 quest_id=MeetingMurakamiQuest new_tag=boss_battle
```

This sets the tag `boss_battle` on `MeetingMurakamiQuest` when the battle is initiated.

On the C# side, tags live in a `HashSet<string>` on `QuestBase.Data`
(`mods/quests_base/src/Quests/QuestBase.cs`):

| Method | Description |
|---|---|
| `AddTag(tag)` | Adds a milestone tag |
| `RemoveTag(tag)` | Revokes a tag (no-op if absent) |
| `ContainsTag(tag)` | Membership check |
| `NotContainsTag(tag)` | Negated membership check |

Scenario `condition:` expressions map onto these directly: `hasTag(...)` calls
`IQuest.ContainsTag`, `notHasTag(...)` calls `IQuest.NotContainsTag` (resolved
by `QuestMethodCondition`, `mods/quests_base/src/Scenes/QuestMethodCondition.cs`).

## Conditional scenarios

Scenarios use quest tags in their `condition:` metadata to decide whether they should run:

```scenario
scene: town_entry
priority: 20
condition: MeetingMurakamiQuest.hasTag("wolf_battle") && MeetingMurakamiQuest.notHasTag("boss_battle")
---
background "town/meetingmurakami/00002-1920617752"
text "[Wolf tracks lead to the door]" key=meeting/after_wolf/tracks
```

This scenario only executes if:
1. The player killed the wolf (`hasTag("wolf_battle")`)
2. The player has NOT yet fought the boss (`notHasTag("boss_battle")`)

## Status progression in practice

The journal reads the current numeric stage (`quest.Data.Status`) and displays
the matching locale key from `StatusToDescription`. Stage advancement happens
via C# or scenario actions:

```scenario
action quest.set_data_status quest_id=AwakeningQuest level=3
```

Lifecycle changes use the named statuses instead:

```scenario
action quest.set_status quest_id=AwakeningQuest status=End
```

To surface a "new journal entry" banner after changing quest state, call:

```scenario
action quest.send_notification quest_id=OldHouseQuest
```

Under the hood this routes to `IQuestExtensions.SendNotification(this IQuest)`
(`mods/quests_base/src/Quests/IQuestExtensions.cs`), which posts a localized
`ui/journal/new - <quest name>` message to the notification banner queue. It
does not modify quest state.

## Events emitted

`quest_status_changed` is published from `QuestServiceImpl.SetStatus`
(`mods/quests_base/src/Actions/QuestServiceImpl.cs`); `quest_tag_changed` is published from
`QuestBase.AddTag` / `QuestBase.RemoveTag` (`mods/quests_base/src/Quests/QuestBase.cs`) — and
only when the tag set actually changed. Both reach GDScript mods through the
ModEventBus string layer (payload keys are snake_case versions of the C#
properties):

| Event key | Payload | Fired when |
|---|---|---|
| `quest_status_changed` | `quest_id`, `old_status`, `new_status` | `QuestService.SetStatus` succeeds |
| `quest_tag_changed` | `quest_id`, `tag`, `added` (bool) | `QuestService.AddTag` (`added=true`) or `RemoveTag` (`added=false`) |

Note: a quest action with an unknown quest ID does nothing — the safe `TryGet`
inside `QuestServiceImpl` logs the miss and skips the mutation, so no event is
published for a quest that doesn't exist.

## Persistence pipeline

`QuestFactory` (`mods/quests_base/src/Quests/QuestFactory.cs`) owns quest instances end-to-end:

- `QuestFactory.Instance.Get(id)` — returns the shared runtime `QuestBase`.
  **Throws `KeyNotFoundException`** when the id was never loaded from mod data
  (typically a typo in a scenario reference).
- `QuestFactory.Instance.GetAll()` — snapshot list of every known quest; used
  by the save system to serialize full quest state.
- `QuestFactory.Instance.SetAll(List<QuestBase>)` — save/restore: looks up each
  entry from the save file by ID and overwrites its `Data` (tags + stage) and
  lifecycle `Status`. Empty/null lists are ignored; entries whose quest no
  longer exists (removed mod, renamed quest) are retained in `QuestFactory.Unresolved` and written back by `QuestsSaveable`, so an ageing save does not silently lose them.
- `QuestFactory.Instance.ResetAll()` — returns every quest to `NotStarted` with
  an empty tag set. Called by `GameData.StartNewGame` because the factory
  singleton survives returning to the main menu and would otherwise leak the
  previous playthrough's progress into the new one.

Scenario-facing `QuestService` methods wrap the factory with an internal safe-get,
so a missing quest in an action command logs an error instead of crashing the
scenario.

## Locale keys

Quest names and descriptions are localizable:

```properties
quest/awakening = Awakening
quest/awakening/0 = Woke up naked in an alley. My head is splitting. Look around — find something to wear.
quest/awakening/1 = Covered myself with a rag and grabbed the book. Then the police showed up. No point arguing — run.
quest/awakening/2 = The whistles died down. I'm at the cemetery. Catch my breath and figure out what to do with the book.
quest/awakening/3 = Read it aloud. Pretty sure I did something. Just what exactly?
quest/awakening/4 = The book started talking. Calls itself Al Azif. Time to get out of here.
quest/awakening/5 = Walking along the river to the villa. I'll wait it out somewhere there.
quest/old_house = The Old House

quest/meeting/murakami = The Girl at the Edge of Town
quest/meeting/murakami/0 = A wolf attacked a house on the outskirts. Investigate.
quest/meeting/murakami/1 = The girl inside needs help. Something's in the back room.
quest/meeting/murakami/2 = She's safe. Murakami joined the party.
```

### Tag locale keys

Tags displayed as journal stamps are resolved through a composite key built
from the quest's `Name` prefix and the tag itself: `{quest.Name}/tag/{tag}`.
For `MeetingMurakamiQuest` (`Name` is `quest/meeting/murakami`) the tag
`go_home` maps to `quest/meeting/murakami/tag/go_home`, so same-named tags in
different quests can carry different translations:

```properties
quest/meeting/murakami/tag/wolf_battle = Wolves defeated
quest/meeting/murakami/tag/boss_battle = Werewolf defeated
quest/meeting/murakami/tag/go_home = Escort home
```

When the composite key is missing, the journal falls back to the raw tag id —
a tag without locale entries still appears as a stamp.


## Adding a new quest

1. Create a JSON file in your mod with the `{ "Kind": "quest", "Items": [ ... ] }` envelope (e.g. `quests/quests.json`)
2. Define `Id`, `Name`, and `StatusToDescription`
3. Add locale keys for all status descriptions
4. Set tags via `action battle.init new_tag=X` or dedicated quest actions
5. Write scenarios with `condition:` that check quest state
6. Scenarios with higher `priority` take precedence when conditions match

## Quest conditions — full expression reference

See Conditions for the complete condition expression language.

Quest state is serialized by `QuestsSaveable` into the `quests_base` slot (`Quests` blob), rather than `__core__`; see Save data.

===== DOC docs/mods/recruiting/index.md: Recruiting =====
The recruiting system manages the post-battle recruit roster. It is a **standalone mod** (`mods/recruiting/src/`): it owns its data, rules, save state and event subscription, and no other mod depends on it. Removing the mod removes recruiting entirely — battles, cooking, story content and the core keep working.

## How it is triggered

The recruiting mod never knows about battles. Whatever system defeats characters publishes a core-neutral event on the global bus:

```csharp
public class CharactersDefeatedEvent : ModEvent
{
    public string   Source       { get; init; }  // "battle", "trap", ...
    public string[] CharacterIds { get; init; }
}
```

- `turn_battle_base` publishes it from the victory screen (`Source = "battle"`) after EXP and loot are processed;
- `RecruitingInitializer` subscribes to it and routes it into `DefeatedCharactersRecruiter`, which resolves the templates through `CharacterFactory`, runs the condition gate, rolls `RecruitmentChance` and adds the winner to the roster.

Because the event type lives in the core and carries ids only, neither side depends on the other, and any mod (or GDScript subscriber via the `characters_defeated` key) can publish or observe group defeats.

## Removing the mod

With `recruiting` disabled:

- `turn_battle_base` still publishes `CharactersDefeatedEvent` — into an empty subscription list;
- `cthangover_game` and `cooking` load normally (they no longer depend on recruiting);
- the save file simply has no `recruiting` slot;
- a core save from an older version opens with an empty roster (no backward-compatible `Recruits` blob is read).

## Data structures

### `Recruit`

| Property | Type | Description |
|---|---|---|
| `ID` | `string` | Unique instance ID (auto-generated Guid) |
| `CharacterID` | `string` | Template character ID (references a character definition) |
| `Properties` | `PropertyData` | Mutable state bag - stores per-recruit custom data |

`Properties` is a free-form key-value bag. The conventional health key is
formalized as the constant `Recruit.PROP_HEALTH` (`"Health"`): `RecruitingData.Add`
seeds it to 10, and behaviours read/write it via
`recruit.Properties.GetInt(Recruit.PROP_HEALTH)` / `SetInt(...)` — e.g. the
built-in `NutritionBehaviour` drains it tick by tick until the recruit is fed.

### `RecruitingData`

The roster is a session singleton — there is exactly one per playthrough:

| Method/Property | Description |
|---|---|
| `Instance` | Static roster owned by the mod (replaces the old `RuntimeData.RecruitingData`) |
| `Data` | `List<Recruit>` - current roster |
| `HasID(id)` | Check if a recruit exists |
| `GetByID(id)` | Look up a recruit by instance ID |
| `HasCharacterID(characterID)` | Check if an enemy template is already captured |
| `GetByCharacterID(characterID)` | Look up the recruit spawned from an enemy template |
| `Add(id, characterID)` | Create a new recruit (uses the given `id`, falling back to a fresh Guid if taken; initialises Health=10 via `Recruit.PROP_HEALTH`; fires `OnConfigure`). **Only one recruit per enemy template is allowed** - a duplicate request returns the existing entry and creates nothing. Returns the `Recruit` instance |
| `Remove(id)` | Remove a recruit from the roster; also detaches it from the party and deletes its placeholder card |

A new game clears `Data` through the `IResettable` the `RecruitingInitializer`
registers in `AppServices`; loading a save replaces it via `RecruitingSaveable`.

## Party integration

The roster itself lives entirely in the mod, but the player can put a captured
recruit into the active party from the mod's own **Recruits** panel (a
`kind:panel` toolbar entry). The panel is a pure view over `RecruitingData`; the
crossing into core data happens in `RecruitPartyBridge`:

| Member | Description |
|---|---|
| `IsInSquad(recruit)` | True when the template ID is in `CharacterData.BattleSet` |
| `AddToSquad(recruit)` | Creates the placeholder `CharacterInfoData` (keyed by `CharacterID`, so the one-per-enemy rule keeps the key unique) via `CharacterData.AddCharacterToParty`, adds it to `BattleSet` and syncs health. Core panels, battle, item targeting and the core save pick the recruit up as a normal party member |
| `RemoveFromSquad(recruit)` | Leaves `BattleSet` but keeps the card, so action assignments and experience survive a temporary leave |
| `Forget(recruit)` | Drops the party entry and deletes the card - used by `RecruitingData.Remove` |
| `SyncToCard(recruit)` | Pushes `Recruit.Properties["Health"]` onto the card, mutating `Attributes.Health` **in place** (battle copies share the object) |
| `SyncFromCard(recruit)` | Pulls the final battle health from the card back into the recruit's property bag |

Synchronization is push-based and owned by the mod: `SyncToCard` runs when the
mod changes its own data (starvation tick, joining the squad) and on save load;
`SyncFromCard` runs on the core-neutral `characters_defeated` event, which marks
the post-battle moment. Outside battle the mod never reads the placeholder card,
so the roster stays the single source of truth.

### `RecruitBehaviourRegistry`

Registry and dispatch hub for recruit lifecycle hooks **and** recruitment
conditions. Implementations are auto-discovered from mod assemblies by
`RecruitingInitializer` — the initializer scans each mod assembly as it is
announced and does a full AppDomain pass at resources-ready. Registration is
id-keyed: a rescan replaces the implementation with the same `Id`, so mod
reloads do not double-dispatch. Dispatch is cross-recruit: every registered
`IRecruitBehaviour` instance receives every active recruit on tick, configure,
and removal events — not just recruits from its own mod.

| Member | Description |
|---|---|
| `Instance` | Static eager singleton (`public static readonly`) - no lazy wrapper, deliberately, to tolerate partial-initialisation during reflection discovery |
| `RegisterAssembly(Assembly)` | Scans one assembly for `IRecruitBehaviour` and `IRecruitCondition` implementations (parameterless constructor required) and registers them by `Id` |
| `RegisterAll()` | Rescans every loaded assembly; used at resources-ready |
| `CanRecruit(Character enemy, RuntimeData runtime)` | Conjunctive gate over all registered conditions - returns true only if **every** `IRecruitCondition` passes |
| `Get(string id)` | Behaviour lookup by its `Id`; linear scan, `null` if absent |
| `All()` | All registered behaviours |
| `OnTick()` | Dispatches `OnTick(recruit, runtime, currentTick)` to every behaviour for every active recruit; lazily ensures the scene timer registration on first call |
| `OnConfigure(Recruit)` | Dispatches `ConfigureRecruit` to every behaviour for a newly added recruit |
| `OnRemove(Recruit)` | Dispatches `OnRemove` to every behaviour for a departing recruit |

### Built-in rules

The mod ships two rules that used to live in content mods:

- `EnslaveCondition` (`IRecruitCondition`, `Id = "enslave"`) — the recruitment gate requires the player to own the `slave_master` skill (checked through `RuntimeData.SkillData`).
- `NutritionBehaviour` (`IRecruitBehaviour`, `Id = "nutrition"`) — seeds `FullnessTime` when a recruit joins and drains `PROP_HEALTH` by 1 per tick once the fullness window has elapsed. The window is a hard-coded `24 * 4 * 60` ticks; at one tick per in-game minute that is 96 in-game hours (4 in-game days), although the source comment calls it "24 hours" — treat the tick constant as authoritative.

Because they live inside `recruiting`, disabling the mod disables the gate and the hunger loop together, without touching the content mods.

## Custom recruit behaviour

Implement `IRecruitBehaviour` to add custom logic on recruit lifecycle events. External mods that implement the interfaces declare `"depends": ["recruiting>=1"]` so their assembly compiles after the mod. Discovery is automatic. Because dispatch is cross-recruit — each behaviour receives every active recruit on each event — a behaviour can also introspect other recruits:

```csharp
public class MyRecruitBehaviour : IRecruitBehaviour
{
    // Unique identifier for RecruitBehaviourRegistry.Get lookup
    public string Id => "my_mod/loyalty";

    public void ConfigureRecruit(Recruit recruit, RuntimeData runtime)
    {
        // Called when a recruit is added - set initial properties;
        // RecruitingData.Add returns the instance, but this hook fires too
        recruit.Properties.SetInt("loyalty", 50);
    }

    public void OnTick(Recruit recruit, RuntimeData runtime, long currentTick)
    {
        // Called every game tick for each active recruit;
        // currentTick is RuntimeData.Time.Tick - use it for periodic logic
    }

    public void OnRemove(Recruit recruit, RuntimeData runtime)
    {
        // Called when a recruit is removed - clean up state
    }
}
```

## `IRecruitCondition`

A gating predicate consulted before any recruitment roll happens
(`mods/recruiting/src/IRecruitCondition.cs`). Conditions receive the defeated
`Character` template and the full `RuntimeData`, so they can inspect stats,
quest state, inventory, or relationship flags:

```csharp
public class MyRecruitCondition : IRecruitCondition
{
    // Unique identifier for debugging and override lookups
    public string Id => "my_mod/level_cap";

    public bool CanRecruit(Character enemy, RuntimeData runtime)
    {
        return enemy.Level <= 5;
    }
}
```

Rules:

- Conditions are auto-discovered by `RecruitingInitializer` - just ship a public class.
- The gate is **conjunctive**: all discovered conditions must return `true`.
  A single `false` blocks recruitment for that enemy - mods compose gates
  without coordinating with each other.
- If no conditions are registered at all, the gate passes vacuously.

## `RecruitTickController`

Periodic tick handler that processes recruit-related updates (e.g. hunger depletion, status regeneration). It registers itself with the scene's `SceneEventController` via `AddTimerTickEventListener` on the first lifecycle call, so no timer cost is paid when no behaviours are loaded.

## Recruitment flow
